<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For the <i>Black Mirror</i> episode, see <a href="/wiki/San_Junipero" title="San Junipero">San Junipero</a>.</div>

.infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style><div class="plainlist"><ul><li>Vocations</li><li><a href="/wiki/Hispanic_Americans" class="mw-redirect" title="Hispanic Americans">Hispanic Americans</a><sup id="cite_ref-en.radiovaticana.va_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-en.radiovaticana.va-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></li><li><a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Junípero Serra y Ferrer</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor" title="Order of Friars Minor">O.F.M.</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;h&#39; in &#39;hi&#39;">h</span><span title="/uː/: &#39;oo&#39; in &#39;goose&#39;">uː</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/iː/: &#39;ee&#39; in &#39;fleece&#39;">iː</span><span title="&#39;p&#39; in &#39;pie&#39;">p</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span></span><span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/ɛr/: &#39;err&#39; in &#39;merry&#39;">ɛr</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>/</a></span></span>; <small>Spanish:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="es-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Spanish" title="Help:IPA/Spanish">[xuˈnipeɾo ˈsera]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a>: <i lang="ca">Juníper Serra i Ferrer</i>; November 24, 1713&#160;&#8211;&#32;August 28, 1784) was a Spanish <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> priest and missionary of the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan Order">Franciscan Order</a>. He is credited with establishing the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Missions_in_the_Sierra_Gorda" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda">Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda</a>, a <a href="/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Site" class="mw-redirect" title="UNESCO World Heritage Site">UNESCO World Heritage Site</a>. He later founded a mission in <a href="/wiki/Baja_California" title="Baja California">Baja California</a> and the first nine of 21 <a href="/wiki/Spanish_missions_in_California" title="Spanish missions in California">Spanish missions in California</a> from <a href="/wiki/San_Diego" title="San Diego">San Diego</a> to <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco" title="San Francisco">San Francisco</a>, in what was then Spanish-occupied <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a> in the Province of <a href="/wiki/The_Californias" title="The Californias">Las Californias</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">New Spain</a>.
</p><p>Serra was <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> on 25 September 1988 in <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican City</a>. Amid denunciations from Native American tribes who accused Serra of presiding over a brutal colonial subjugation,<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> Serra on 23 September 2015 at the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_the_National_Shrine_of_the_Immaculate_Conception" title="Basilica of the National Shrine of the Immaculate Conception">Basilica of the National Shrine of the Immaculate Conception</a> in <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, during his <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis%27_visit_to_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Francis&#39; visit to the United States">first visit to the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Serra's missionary efforts earned him the title of "Apostle of California".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Both before and after his canonization, Serra's reputation and missionary work during the Spanish occupation have been condemned by critics, who cite alleged mandatory conversions to Catholicism, followed by abuse of the Native American converts.<sup id="cite_ref-PBS_-_THE_WEST_-_Junipero_Serra_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PBS_-_THE_WEST_-_Junipero_Serra-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_(4).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg/170px-Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg/255px-Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg/340px-Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_%284%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2712" data-file-height="3616" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Casa_natal_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra_a_Petra_(4).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra's birthplace in <a href="/wiki/Petra,_Mallorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Petra, Mallorca">Petra</a> on the island of <a href="/wiki/Mallorca" title="Mallorca">Mallorca</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Balearic_Islands" title="Balearic Islands">Balearic Islands</a>.</div></div></div> 
<p>Serra was born <b>Miquel Josep Serra i Ferrer</b><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="Reliable source needed for this spelling as a birth name (June 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> (this name is <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Castilian</a> it is Miguel José Serra Ferrer) in the village of <a href="/wiki/Petra,_Mallorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Petra, Mallorca">Petra</a> on the island of <a href="/wiki/Mallorca" title="Mallorca">Mallorca</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Balearic_Islands" title="Balearic Islands">Balearic Islands</a> off the Mediterranean coast of Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> His father Antonio Nadal Serra and mother Margarita Rosa Ferrer were married in 1707.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By age seven, Miquel was working the fields with his parents, helping cultivate wheat and beans, and tending the cattle. But he showed a special interest in visiting the local <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> <a href="/wiki/Priory" title="Priory">friary</a> at the church of San Bernardino within a block of the Serra family house. Attending the <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">friars</a>' primary school at the church, Miquel learned reading, writing, mathematics, Latin, religion and <a href="/wiki/Liturgical_music" title="Liturgical music">liturgical song</a>, especially Gregorian chant. Gifted with a good voice, he eagerly took to vocal music. The friars sometimes let him join the community choir and sing at special church feasts. Miquel and his father Antonio often visited the friary for friendly chats with the Franciscans.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At age 16, Miquel's parents enrolled him in a Franciscan school in the capital city, <a href="/wiki/Palma,_Majorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Palma, Majorca">Palma de Majorca</a>, where he studied philosophy. A year later, he became a <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novice</a> in the Franciscan order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHackel201327–31_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHackel201327–31-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Joins_Franciscan_order">Joins Franciscan order</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg/220px-Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg/330px-Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg/440px-Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3616" data-file-height="2712" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Placa_en_record_del_bateig_de_Fra_Jun%C3%ADper_Serra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Memorial to Serra's baptism at the <a href="https://ca.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esgl%C3%A9sia_de_Sant_Pere_de_Petra" class="extiw" title="ca:Església de Sant Pere de Petra">Church of Sant Pere de Petra</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>On September 14, 1730, some two months before his 17th birthday, Serra entered the Franciscan Order at Palma,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHackel201331_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHackel201331-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> specifically, the <a href="/wiki/Alcantarine" class="mw-redirect" title="Alcantarine">Alcantarine</a> branch of the Friars Minor, a reform movement in the Order. The slight and frail Serra now embarked on his novitiate period, a rigorous year of preparation to become a full member of the Franciscan Order. He was given the <a href="/wiki/Religious_name" title="Religious name">religious name</a> of Junípero in honor of <a href="/wiki/Brother_Juniper" class="mw-redirect" title="Brother Juniper">Brother Juniper</a>, who had been among the first Franciscans and a companion of <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SerraClub_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SerraClub-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The young Junípero, along with his fellow novices, vowed to scorn property and comfort, and to remain <a href="/wiki/Celibacy" title="Celibacy">celibate</a>. He still had seven years to go to become an <a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">ordained</a> <a href="/wiki/Catholic_priest" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic priest">Catholic priest</a>. He immersed himself in rigorous studies of logic, metaphysics, cosmology, and theology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHackel201340_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHackel201340-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The daily routine at the friary followed a rigid schedule: prayers, meditation, choir singing, physical chores, spiritual readings, and instruction. The friars would wake up every midnight for another round of chants. Serra's superiors discouraged letters and visitors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198515_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198515-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In his free time, he avidly read stories about Franciscan friars roaming the provinces of Spain and around the world to win new souls for the church, often suffering <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyrs" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian martyrs">martyrdom</a> in the process. 
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG/170px-2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="270" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG/255px-2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG/340px-2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG 2x" data-file-width="526" data-file-height="835" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:2007-08-15_Spain_Mallorca_Palma_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_01.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument to Serra in <a href="/wiki/Palma_de_Mallorca" title="Palma de Mallorca">Palma de Mallorca</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1737, Serra became a priest, and three years later earned an ecclesiastical license to teach philosophy at the Convento de San Francisco. His philosophy course, including over 60 students, lasted three years. Among his students were fellow future missionaries <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pal%C3%B3u" title="Francisco Palóu">Francisco Palóu</a> and <a href="/wiki/Juan_Cresp%C3%AD" title="Juan Crespí">Juan Crespí</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> When the course ended in 1743, Serra told his students: "I desire nothing more from you than this, that when the news of my death shall have reached your ears, I ask you to say for the benefit of my soul: 'May he rest in peace.' Nor shall I omit to do the same for you so that all of us will attain the goal for which we have been created."<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra was considered intellectually brilliant by his peers. He received a doctorate in theology from the Lullian College (founded in the 14th century by <a href="/wiki/Ramon_Lull" class="mw-redirect" title="Ramon Lull">Ramon Lull</a> for the training of Franciscan missionaries) in Palma de Majorca, where he also occupied the <a href="/wiki/Duns_Scotus" title="Duns Scotus">Duns Scotus</a> chair of philosophy until he joined the missionary <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">College of San Fernando de Mexico</a> in 1749.<sup id="cite_ref-Engelhardt_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Engelhardt-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Serra's last five years on the island of Majorca, drought and plague afflicted his home village of Petra. Serra sometimes went home from Palma for brief visits to his parents—now separated—and gave them some financial support. On one occasion he was called home to anoint his seriously ill father with the <a href="/wiki/Last_rites" title="Last rites">last rites</a>. In one of his final visits to Petra, Serra found his younger sister Juana María near death.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1748, Serra and Palóu confided to each other their desire to become missionaries. Serra, now 35, was assured a prestigious career as priest and scholar if he stayed in Majorca; but he set his sights firmly on pagan lands. Applying to the colonial bureaucracy in Madrid, Serra requested that both he and Palóu embark on a foreign mission. After weathering some administrative obstacles, they received permission and set sail for <a href="/wiki/C%C3%A1diz" title="Cádiz">Cádiz</a>, the port of departure for Spain's colonies in the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHackel201359_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHackel201359-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While waiting to set sail, Serra wrote a long letter to a colleague back in Majorca, urging him to console Serra's parents—now in their 70s—over their only son's pending departure. "They [my parents] will learn to see how sweet is His yoke," Serra wrote, "and that He will change for them the sorrow they may now experience into great happiness. Now is not the time to muse or fret over the happenings of life but rather to be conformed entirely to the will of God, striving to prepare themselves for that happy death which of all the things of life is our principal concern."<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>  Serra asked his colleague to read this letter to his parents, who had never attended school.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ministry_in_the_Americas">Ministry in the Americas</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg/170px-Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg/255px-Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg/340px-Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2167" data-file-height="3250" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Fray_Junipero_Serra_en_Tancoyol.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra monument in <a href="https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipio_de_Jalpan_de_Serra" class="extiw" title="es:Municipio de Jalpan de Serra">Jalpan de Serra</a>, a city named after Serra in <a href="/wiki/Quer%C3%A9taro" title="Querétaro">Querétaro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>In 1749, Serra and the Franciscan missionary team landed in <a href="/wiki/Veracruz_(city)" title="Veracruz (city)">Veracruz</a>, on the Gulf coast of New Spain (now Mexico). To get from Veracruz to Mexico City, Serra and his Franciscan companions took the Camino Real (English: <span lang="en">royal path</span>), a rough road stretching from sea level through tropical forests, dry plains, high plateaus and volcanic sierra mountains to an altitude of 7,400 feet (2,300 meters). Royal officials provided horses for the 20 Franciscan friars to ride up the Camino Real. All accepted the offer, except for Serra and one companion, a friar from <a href="/wiki/Andalusia" title="Andalusia">Andalusia</a>. Strictly following the rule of his patron saint <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a> that friars "must not ride on horseback unless compelled by manifest necessity or infirmity," Serra insisted on walking to Mexico City. He and his fellow friar set out on the Camino Real with no money or guide, carrying only their <a href="/wiki/Breviary" title="Breviary">breviaries</a>. They trusted in Providence and the hospitality of local people along the way.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>During the trek Serra's left foot swelled up, and a burning itch tormented him. Arriving at a farm at day's end, he could hardly stand. He attributed the swelling to a mosquito bite. His discomfort caused him to stay over at the farm another night, during which he scratched his foot and leg to excess, desperately trying to relieve the itch. The next morning his leg was raw and bleeding. This wound plagued Serra for the rest of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hobbling into Mexico City, Serra joined up with his fellow friars at the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">College of San Fernando de Mexico</a>, a specialized training center and regional headquarters for Franciscan missionaries. Serra requested that he do his novitiate year again—despite his academic prestige, and the fact that the college's novices were far younger men. Though his request was declined, Serra insisted on living as a novice at San Fernando: "This learned university professor ... would often eat more sparingly in order to replace the student whose turn it was to read to the community. Or he would humbly carry trays and wait on tables with the lay brothers."<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mission_in_the_Sierra_Gorda">Mission in the Sierra Gorda</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa,_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg/170px-Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="302" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg/255px-Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg/340px-Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa%2C_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2268" data-file-height="4032" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_del_Agua_de_Landa,_Landa_de_Matamoros.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra founded the five <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Missions_in_the_Sierra_Gorda_of_Quer%C3%A9taro" title="Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda of Querétaro">Missions of the Sierra Gorda</a> in <a href="/wiki/Quer%C3%A9taro" title="Querétaro">Querétaro</a>, Mexico between 1750–60. (Santa María del Agua de Landa pictured).</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Missions_in_the_Sierra_Gorda" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda">Sierra Gorda Indian missions</a>, some 90 miles north of <a href="/wiki/Quer%C3%A9taro,_Quer%C3%A9taro" class="mw-redirect" title="Querétaro, Querétaro">Santiago de Querétaro</a>, were nestled in a vast region of jagged mountains, home of the <a href="/wiki/Pame_people" title="Pame people">Pame people</a> and a scattering of Spanish colonists. The Pames—who centuries earlier had built a civilization with temples, idols and priests—lived mainly by gathering and hunting, but also pursued agriculture. Many groups among them, adopting mobile guerrilla tactics, had eluded conquest by the Spanish military.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Serra and Palóu, arriving at the village of <a href="/wiki/Jalpan_de_Serra" title="Jalpan de Serra">Jalpan</a>, found the mission in disarray: The parishioners, numbering fewer than a thousand, were attending neither confession nor <a href="/wiki/Mass_(liturgy)" title="Mass (liturgy)">Mass</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198549–50_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198549–50-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The two missionaries set about learning the Pame language from a Mexican who had lived among the Pames. But the statement by Palóu that Serra translated the catechism into the Pame language is questionable, as Serra himself later admitted he had great difficulties learning indigenous languages.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra involved Pames parishioners in the ritual reenactment of Jesus' forced death march. Erecting 14 stations, Serra led the procession himself, carrying an extremely heavy cross. At each station, the procession paused for a prayer, and at the end Serra sermonized on the sufferings and death of Jesus. On <a href="/wiki/Holy_Thursday" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Thursday">Holy Thursday</a>, 12 Pames elders reenacted the roles of the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostles</a>. Serra, in the role of Jesus, washed their feet and then, after the service, dined with them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy1985501_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy1985501-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra also tackled the practical side of mission administration. Working with the college of San Fernando, he had cattle, goats, sheep, and farming tools brought to the Sierra Gorda mission. Palóu supervised the farm labor of men of the mission; the women learned spinning, sewing and knitting. Their products were collected and rationed to the mission residents, according to personal needs. Christian Pames sold their surplus products in nearby trading centers, under the friars' supervision to protect them from cheaters. Pames who adapted successfully to mission life received their own parcels of land to raise corn, beans and pumpkins, and sometimes received oxen and seeds as well.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Within two years, Serra had made inroads against the Pames' traditional belief system. On his 1752 visit from the Sierra Gorda mission to the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a> in Mexico City, Serra joyfully carried a goddess statue presented to him by Christian Pames. The statue, showing the face of Cachum, mother of the sun, had been erected on a hilltop shrine where some Pame chiefs lay buried.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198552_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198552-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG/220px-Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG/330px-Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG/440px-Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3203" data-file-height="2097" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Petra_-_Mallorca_-_Missions_de_Sierra_Gorda_.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Azulejos" class="mw-redirect" title="Azulejos">Azulejos</a> depicting the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Missions_in_the_Sierra_Gorda_of_Quer%C3%A9taro" title="Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda of Querétaro">Sierra Gorda Missions</a>, which Serra founded between 1750–60.</div></div></div>
<p>Back in the Sierra Gorda, Serra faced a conflict between Spanish soldiers, settlers, and mission natives or "Indians". Following a Spanish military victory over the Pames in 1743, Spanish authorities had sent not only Franciscan missionaries, but also Spanish/Mexican soldiers and their families into the Sierra Gorda. The soldiers had the job of pursuing runaway mission Indians and securing the region for the Spanish crown. But the soldiers' land claims clashed with mission lands that Christian Pames were working.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Some of the soldiers' families tried to establish a town, and the officer in charge of their deployment approved their plan. The Pames objected, threatening to defend their lands by force if necessary. Soldiers and settlers let their cattle graze on Christian Pames' farmlands and bullied Pames into working for them. Serra and the College of San Fernando sided with the Pames—citing the <a href="/wiki/Laws_of_the_Indies" title="Laws of the Indies">Laws of the Indies</a>, which banned colonial settlements in mission territories.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Viceroy" title="Viceroy">viceroy</a>, Spain's highest official in Mexico, suspended the intrusive colony. But the townspeople protested and stayed put. The government set up commissions and looked into alternative sites for the colony. It ordered the settlers to keep their cattle out of the Pames' fields, and to pay the Pames fairly for their labor (with the friars supervising payment). After a protracted legal struggle, the settlers moved out, and in 1755 the Pames and friars reclaimed their land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198555–56_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198555–56-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Crowning his Sierra Gorda mission, Serra oversaw the construction of a splendid church in <a href="/wiki/Jalpan_de_Serra" title="Jalpan de Serra">Jalpan</a>. Gathering masons, carpenters, and other skilled craftsmen from Mexico City, Serra employed Christian Pames in seasonal construction work over the course of seven years to complete the church. Serra pitched in himself, carrying wooden beams and applying mortar between the stones forming the church walls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198555_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198555-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Work_for_the_Inquisition">Work for the Inquisition</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Junipero_Serra.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Junipero_Serra.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Junipero_Serra.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="296" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Junipero_Serra.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Junipero_Serra.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Junipero_Serra.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Junipero_Serra.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2585" data-file-height="3480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Junipero_Serra.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Engraving depicting Serra's as an evangelizer from 1787.</div></div></div>
<p>During his 1752 visit to Mexico City, Serra sent a request from the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a> to the local headquarters of the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Spanish Inquisition</a>. He asked that an inquisitor be appointed to preside over the Sierra Gorda. The next day, Inquisition officials appointed Serra himself as inquisitor for the whole region—adding that he could exercise his powers anywhere he did missionary work in New Spain, as long as there was no regular Inquisition official in the region.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1752, Serra filed a report to the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Spanish Inquisition</a> in Mexico City from <a href="/wiki/Jalpan_de_Serra" title="Jalpan de Serra">Jalpan</a>, on "evidences of witchcraft in the Sierra Gorda missions." He denounced several Christian non-Indians who lived in and around the mission for "the most detestable and horrible crimes of sorcery, witchcraft and devil worship. ... If it is necessary to specify one of the persons guilty of such crimes, I accuse by name a certain Melchora de los Reyes Acosta, a married mulattress, an inhabitant of the said mission..... In these last days a certain Cayetana, a very clever Mexican woman of said mission, married to one Pérez, a mulatto, has confessed—she, being observed and accused of similar crimes, having been held under arrest by us for some days past—that in the mission there is a large congregation of [Christian non-Indians], although some Indians also join them, and that these persons, ... flying through the air at night, are in the habit of meeting in a cave on a hill near a ranch called El Saucillo, in the center of said missions, where they worship and make sacrifice to the demons who appear visibly there in the guise of young goats and various other things of that nature. ... If such evil is not attacked, the horrible corruption will spread among these poor [Indian] neophytes who are in our charge."<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to modern Franciscan historians, this report by Serra to the Inquisition is the only letter of his that has survived from eight years of mission work in the Sierra Gorda.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Serra's first biographer, <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pal%C3%B3u" title="Francisco Palóu">Francisco Palóu</a>, wrote that Serra, in his role of inquisitor, had to work in many parts of Mexico and travel long distances. Yet the Archivo General de la Nación in Mexico City, with over a thousand volumes of indexed documents on the Inquisition, apparently contains only two references to Serra's work for the Inquisition following his 1752 appointment: his preaching in Oaxaca in 1764, and his partial handling of the case of a Sierra Gorda mulatto accused of sorcery in 1766.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198556_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198556-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1758, Serra returned to the College of San Fernando. Over the next nine years he worked in the college's administrative offices, and as a missionary and inquisitor in the dioceses of Mexico, Puebla, Oaxaca, Valladolid, and Guadalajara.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Physical_self-punishment">Physical self-punishment</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG/170px-JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="254" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG/255px-JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG/340px-JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="3872" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:JSerraPlaqueConc%C3%A1.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Plaque honoring Serra at <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Missions_in_the_Sierra_Gorda_of_Quer%C3%A9taro#Missions" title="Franciscan Missions in the Sierra Gorda of Querétaro">Mission San Miguel Concá</a> in <a href="/wiki/Arroyo_Seco_Municipality" title="Arroyo Seco Municipality">Arroyo Seco</a>, Querétaro.</div></div></div>
<p>Emulating an earlier Franciscan missionary and saint, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Solanus" title="Francis Solanus">Francisco Solano</a>, Serra made a habit of punishing himself physically, to purify his spirit. He wore a sackcloth spiked with bristles, or a coat interwoven with broken pieces of wire, under his gray friar's outer garment.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In his austere cell, Serra kept a chain of sharp pointed iron links hanging on the wall beside his bed, to whip himself at night when sinful thoughts ran through his mind. His nightly self-flagellations at the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a> caught the ears of some of his fellow friars. In his letters to his Franciscan companions, Serra often referred to himself as a "sinner" and a "most unworthy priest."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In one of his sermons in Mexico City, while exhorting his listeners to repent their sins, Serra took out his chain, bared his shoulders and started whipping himself. Many parishioners, roused by the spectacle, began sobbing. Finally, a man climbed to the pulpit, took the chain from Serra's hand and began whipping <i>him</i>self, declaring: "I am the sinner who is ungrateful to God who ought to do penance for my many sins, and not the padre [Serra], who is a saint." The man kept whipping himself until he collapsed. After receiving the last sacraments, he later died from the ordeal.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg/170px-Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg/255px-Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg/340px-Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="375" data-file-height="500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pla%C3%A7aPareSerra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Plaza de Junípero Serra in <a href="/wiki/Petra,_Mallorca" class="mw-redirect" title="Petra, Mallorca">Petra, Mallorca</a>, Spain.</div></div></div>
<p>During other sermons on the theme of repentance, Serra would hoist a large stone in one hand and, while clutching a crucifix in the other, smash the stone against his chest. Many of his listeners feared that he would strike himself dead. Later, Serra suffered chest pains and shortness of breath; Palóu suggests that Serra's self-inflicted bruises were the cause. While preaching of hell and damnation, Serra would sear his flesh with a four-pronged candle flame—emulating a famed Franciscan preacher, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Capistrano" title="John of Capistrano">John of Capistrano</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198559_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198559-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Palóu described this as "quite violent, painful, and dangerous towards wounding his chest."<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra did not stand alone among Catholic missionaries in displaying self-punishment at the pulpit. The more zealous Franciscan and Jesuit missionaries did likewise. But few took it to the extremes that Serra did. The regulations of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a> said that self-punishment should never be carried to the point of permanently incapacitating oneself.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="King_Carlos_expels_the_Jesuits">King Carlos expels the Jesuits</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista,_Puerto_Rico.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg/170px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="113" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg/255px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg/340px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista%2C_Puerto_Rico.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4800" data-file-height="3200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_lived_in_this_house_in_San_Juan_Bautista,_Puerto_Rico.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Plaque at Serra's house in <a href="/wiki/San_Juan,_Puerto_Rico" title="San Juan, Puerto Rico">San Juan, Puerto Rico</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>On June 24, 1767, the Viceroy of New Spain, <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Francisco_de_Croix,_marqu%C3%A9s_de_Croix" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlos Francisco de Croix, marqués de Croix">Carlos Francisco de Croix</a>, read a Spanish royal decree to Mexico's archbishop and assembled church officials:  "Repair with an armed force to the houses of the Jesuits. Seize the persons of all of them and, within 24 hours, transport them as prisoners to the port of Veracruz. Cause to be sealed the records of said houses and records of such persons without allowing them to remove anything but their breviaries and such garments as are absolutely necessary for their journey. If after the embarkation there should be found one Jesuit in that district, even if ill or dying, you shall suffer the penalty of death."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy19856–7_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy19856–7-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Spain's king <a href="/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain" title="Charles III of Spain">Carlos III</a> had plotted the <a href="/wiki/Suppression_of_the_Society_of_Jesus" title="Suppression of the Society of Jesus">expulsion of Jesuits</a> throughout his empire five months earlier.
</p><p>On the <a href="/wiki/Baja_California_peninsula" title="Baja California peninsula">Baja California peninsula</a>, newly appointed governor <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Gaspar de Portolá</a> had to notify and remove the Jesuits from the chain of missions they had developed in forbidding territory over 70 years. By February 1768, Portolá gathered the 16 Baja Jesuit missionaries in <a href="/wiki/Loreto,_Baja_California_Sur" title="Loreto, Baja California Sur">Loreto</a>, from where they sailed to mainland Mexico for deportation. Sympathetic to the Jesuits, Portolá treated them kindly even as he removed them under the king's orders.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="President_of_missions_of_the_Californias">President of missions of the Californias</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_(Lleida).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg/220px-Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="281" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg/330px-Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg/440px-Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_%28Lleida%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="927" data-file-height="1184" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Retrat_Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A0_(Lleida).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Gaspar de Portolá</a>'s appointment as <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_the_Californias" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor of the Californias">Governor of the Californias</a> in 1767 coincided with Serra's appointment as chief of the missions in <a href="/wiki/The_Californias" title="The Californias">the Californias</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Into the vacuum created by the <a href="/wiki/Suppression_of_the_Society_of_Jesus#Jesuits_expelled_from_Mexico_(New_Spain)" title="Suppression of the Society of Jesus">Jesuits' expulsion from Mexico</a>, stepped Franciscan missionaries. In July 1767, the guardian of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a> appointed Serra president of the missions of <a href="/wiki/Baja_California" title="Baja California">Baja California</a>, heading a group of 15 Franciscan friars; <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pal%C3%B3u" title="Francisco Palóu">Francisco Palóu</a> served as his second in command.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Jesuit priests had developed 13 missions on that long and arid peninsula over seven decades. Two Jesuits had died at the hands of Indians in the revolt of 1734–36.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In March 1768, Serra and his missionary team boarded a Spanish <a href="/wiki/Sloop" title="Sloop">sloop</a> at <a href="/wiki/San_Blas,_Nayarit" title="San Blas, Nayarit">San Blas</a>, on Mexico's Pacific coast. Sailing over 200 miles up the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_California" title="Gulf of California">Gulf of California</a>, they landed at <a href="/wiki/Loreto,_Baja_California_Sur" title="Loreto, Baja California Sur">Loreto</a> two weeks later. <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Gaspar de Portolá</a>, governor of Las Californias, welcomed them at the <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto_Conch%C3%B3" title="Misión de Nuestra Señora de Loreto Conchó">Loreto mission</a>, founded by Jesuits in 1697. While he gave control of the church to Serra, Portolá controlled the living quarters and rationed out food to the friars, charging their costs to the mission.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra and Palóu found—to their unpleasant surprise—that they ruled only on spiritual matters: everyday management of the mission remained in the hands of the military, who had occupied the Baja missions since evicting the Jesuits. In August 1768, New Spain's inspector general <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_G%C3%A1lvez" class="mw-redirect" title="José de Gálvez">José de Gálvez</a>, displeased with the sloppy military administration of the Baja missions, ordered them turned over fully to the Franciscan friars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198564–68_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198564–68-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Franciscans found that the Indian population in the Baja California mission territories had dwindled to about 7,150. By the time the Franciscans had moved north and turned the missions over to Dominican friars in 1772, the Indian population had decreased to about 5,000. "If it goes on at this rate," wrote Palóu, "in a short time Baja California will come to an end." Epidemics, especially syphilis introduced by Spanish troops, were wasting the Indians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198566_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198566-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> But Palóu attributed the ravages of syphilis to God's retribution for the Indians' murder of the two Jesuit priests over 30 years earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1768 <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_G%C3%A1lvez" class="mw-redirect" title="José de Gálvez">José de Gálvez</a>, inspector general of New Spain, decided to send explorers and locate missions in Alta (upper) California. Gálvez aimed both to Christianize the extensive Indian populations and serve Spain's strategic interest by preventing Russian explorations and possible claims to North America's Pacific coast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198569–72_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198569–72-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Gálvez chose Serra to head the missionary team in the California expedition. Serra, now 55, eagerly seized the chance to harvest thousands of pagan souls in lands previously untouched by the church.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg/170px-Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg/255px-Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg/340px-Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Escultura_de_Fray_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_ubicada_frente_a_la_Curia_de_Quer%C3%A9taro.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument to Serra in <a href="/wiki/Quer%C3%A9taro_City" title="Querétaro City">Querétaro City</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>But as the expedition gathered in <a href="/wiki/Loreto,_Baja_California_Sur" title="Loreto, Baja California Sur">Loreto</a>, Serra's foot and leg infection had become almost crippling. The commander, <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Gaspar de Portolá</a>, tried to dissuade him from joining the expedition, and wrote to <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_G%C3%A1lvez" class="mw-redirect" title="José de Gálvez">Gálvez</a> about Serra's condition. Serra's fellow friar and former student <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pal%C3%B3u" title="Francisco Palóu">Francisco Palóu</a> also became concerned, gently suggesting to Serra that he stay in Baja California and let the younger and stronger Palóu make the journey to San Diego in his place. Serra rebuffed both Portolá's and Palóu's doubts. He chided Palóu for his suggestion: "Let us not speak of that. I have placed all my confidence in God, of whose goodness I hope that He will grant me to reach not only San Diego to raise the standard of the Holy Cross in that port, but also Monterey."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198575_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198575-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra suggested that the Portolá party set off without him; he would follow and meet up with them on the way to Alta California. He then assigned friar Miguel de la Campa as chaplain to the <a href="/wiki/Portol%C3%A1_expedition" title="Portolá expedition">Portolá expedition</a>, which set out from <a href="/wiki/Loreto,_Baja_California_Sur" title="Loreto, Baja California Sur">Loreto</a> on March 9, 1769. Spending holy week at <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto_Conch%C3%B3" title="Misión de Nuestra Señora de Loreto Conchó">mission Loreto</a>, Serra set out on March 28. "From my mission of Loreto," wrote Serra, "I took along no more provisions for so long a journey than a loaf of bread and a piece of cheese. For I was there [at mission Loreto] a whole year, in economic matters, as a mere guest to receive the crumbs of the royal soldier commissioner, whose liberality at my departure did not extend beyond the aforementioned articles."<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two servants—one named José María Vergerano, a 20-year-old from <a href="/wiki/Magdalena_Municipality,_Jalisco" class="mw-redirect" title="Magdalena Municipality, Jalisco">Magdalena</a>, the other a soldier guard—accompanied Serra on his journey from Loreto, as he rode on a feeble mule. On April 28, 1769, Serra arrived at <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_San_Francisco_Borja" title="Misión San Francisco Borja">mission San Borja</a>, where he received a warm welcome from friar <a href="/wiki/Ferm%C3%ADn_Lasu%C3%A9n" class="mw-redirect" title="Fermín Lasuén">Fermín Lasuén</a>. Founded just seven years before by the Jesuit <a href="/wiki/Wenceslaus_Linck" title="Wenceslaus Linck">Wenceslaus Linck</a>, mission San Borja sat in an unusually arid region of Baja California. Continuing north, Serra stopped on May 5 to celebrate a <a href="/wiki/Mass_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass (Catholic Church)">Mass</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_the_Ascension" title="Feast of the Ascension">feast of the Ascension</a> in the deserted church at <a href="/wiki/Visita_de_Calamaju%C3%A9" title="Visita de Calamajué">Calamajué</a>, scarcely more than a ruined hut. The next morning he arrived at <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_los_%C3%81ngeles" title="Misión Santa María de los Ángeles">Santa María</a>, where he met up with <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Portolá</a>, friar Miguel de la Campa and several members of their party. In this arid region, whose alkaline land resisted cultivation, lived the "poorest of all" the Indians Serra had encountered in Mexico. On Sunday May 7, Serra celebrated high Mass and preached a sermon at the mission church on the frontier of Spanish Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198578_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198578-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Founding_Mission_Velicat.C3.A1"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Founding_Mission_Velicatá">Founding Mission Velicatá</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg/220px-Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg/330px-Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg/440px-Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg 2x" data-file-width="648" data-file-height="401" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto._Siglo_XVIII.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra prepared his evangelizing mission of <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a> at <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_de_Nuestra_Se%C3%B1ora_de_Loreto_Conch%C3%B3" title="Misión de Nuestra Señora de Loreto Conchó">Mission Loreto</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Baja_California" title="Baja California">Baja California</a>, in 1768–69.</div></div></div>
<p>After leaving <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_de_los_%C3%81ngeles" title="Misión Santa María de los Ángeles">Mission Santa María</a>, Serra urged <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Portolá</a> to move ahead of the slow pack train, so they could reach Velicatá in time for <a href="/wiki/Pentecost" title="Pentecost">Pentecost</a> the next day. Portolá agreed, so the small group traveled all day May 13 to reach Velicatá by late evening. The advanced guard of the party greeted them there.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On Pentecost day, May 14, 1769, Serra founded his first mission, <a href="/wiki/Misi%C3%B3n_San_Fernando_Rey_de_Espa%C3%B1a_de_Velicat%C3%A1" title="Misión San Fernando Rey de España de Velicatá">Misión San Fernando Rey de España de Velicatá</a>, in a mud hut that had served as a makeshift church when friar <a href="/wiki/Ferm%C3%ADn_Lasu%C3%A9n" class="mw-redirect" title="Fermín Lasuén">Fermín Lasuén</a> had traveled up on Easter to conduct the sacraments for the <a href="/wiki/Fernando_Rivera_y_Moncada" title="Fernando Rivera y Moncada">Fernando Rivera</a> expedition, the overland party that had preceded the Portolá party. The founding celebration took place "with all the neatness of holy poverty," in Serra's words. Smoke from the soldiers' guns, fired in repeated volleys, served as incense.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198579_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198579-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The new mission lacked Indians to convert. A few days later, friar Miguel de la Campa notified Serra that a few natives had arrived. Serra joyously rushed out to welcome twelve Indian, men and boys. "Then I saw what I could hardly begin to believe when I read about it," wrote Serra. "...&#160;namely, that they go about entirely naked like Adam in paradise before the fall. ... We treated with them for a long time; and although they saw all of us clothed, they nevertheless showed not the least trace of shame in their manner of nudity." Serra placed both hands upon their heads as a token of paternal affection. He then handed them figs, which they ate immediately. One of the Indian men gave Serra roasted <a href="/wiki/Agave" title="Agave">agave</a> stalks and four fishes. In return, Portolá and his soldiers offered tobacco leaves and various food items.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198580_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198580-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Through a Christian Indian interpreter, Serra told the Indians that de la Campa would stay at the mission to serve them. According to Stephen Hyslop, "[Serra's] goal and that of his fellow friars was not to confirm Indians &#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; in their seeming innocence, like 'Adam in the garden, before sin', but to make them aware of their sins and move them to repent."<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The motive behind gifts of food, tobacco, and the like was, "in the words of Serra's colleague and biographer, Father Francisco Palóu, spiritual conquest meant enticing Indians &#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; with food and clothing, by which means they could be indoctrinated as Christians and 'gradually acquire a knowledge of what is spiritually good and evil' ".<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_(Statue).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG/220px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG/330px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG/440px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_%28Statue%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2250" data-file-height="2952" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_(Statue).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A statue of Serra by <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Tilden" title="Douglas Tilden">Douglas Tilden</a> formerly installed in <a href="/wiki/Golden_Gate_Park" title="Golden Gate Park">Golden Gate Park</a>, <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco" title="San Francisco">San Francisco</a> before it was removed during the <a href="/wiki/George_Floyd_protests" title="George Floyd protests">George Floyd protests</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Back on the road, Serra found it very difficult to stay on his feet because "my left foot had become very inflamed, a painful condition which I have suffered for a year or more. Now this inflammation has reached halfway up my leg." Portolá again tried to persuade Serra to withdraw from the expedition, offering to "have you carried back to the first mission where you can recuperate, and we will continue our journey." Serra countered that "God ... has given me the strength to come so far. ... Even though I should die on the way, I shall not turn back. They can bury me wherever they wish and I shall gladly be left among the pagans, if it be God's will." Portolá had a stretcher prepared, so that Christian Indians traveling with the expedition could carry Serra along the trail.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Not wishing to burden his traveling companies, Serra departed from his usual practice of avoiding medicines: he asked one of the <a href="/wiki/Arriero" title="Arriero">muleteers</a>, Juan Antonio Coronel, if he could prepare a remedy for his foot and leg wound. When Coronel objected that he knew only how to heal animals' wounds, Serra rejoined: "Well then, son, just imagine that I am an animal. ... Make me the same remedy that you would apply to an animal." Coronel then crushed some <a href="/wiki/Tallow" title="Tallow">tallow</a> between stones and mixed it with green desert herbs. After heating the mix, he applied it to Serra's foot and leg. The next morning, Serra felt "much improved and I celebrated Mass. ... I was enabled to make the daily trek just as if I did not have any ailment. ... There is no swelling but only the itching which I feel at times."<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The expedition still had 300 miles (480 kilometers) to travel to San Diego. They passed through desert terrain into oak savanna in June, often camping and sleeping under large oaks. From a high hill on June 20, their advance scouts saw the Pacific Ocean in the distance. Reaching its shores that evening, the party called the spot Ensenada de Todos Santos (All Saints' Cove, today simply <a href="/wiki/Ensenada,_Baja_California" title="Ensenada, Baja California">Ensenada</a>). They now had less than 80 miles (130 kilometers) to reach San Diego.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Pressing north, they stayed close to the ocean. On June 23, they came upon a large Indian village where they enjoyed a pleasant stopover. The natives appeared healthy, robust and friendly, immediately repeating the Spanish words they heard. Some danced for the party, offering them fish and mussels. "We were all enamored of them," wrote Serra. "In fact, all the pagans have pleased me, but these in particular have stolen my heart."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198584_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198584-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Indians now encountered by the party near the coast appeared well-fed and more eager to receive cloth than food. On June 25, as the party struggled to cross a series of ravines, they noticed many Indians following them. When they camped for the night, the Indians pressed close. Whenever Serra placed his hands on their heads, they placed theirs on his. Coveting cloth, some begged Serra for the friar's habit he wore. Several women passed Serra's spectacles around with delight from hand to hand, until one man dashed off with them. Serra's companions rushed to recover them, the only pair of spectacles Serra possessed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198585–86_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeNeviMoholy198585–86-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arrival_in_San_Diego">Arrival in San Diego</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Old_Town,_San_Diego,_CA,_USA_-_panoramio_(12).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg/220px-Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="294" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg/330px-Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg/440px-Old_Town%2C_San_Diego%2C_CA%2C_USA_-_panoramio_%2812%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2712" data-file-height="3630" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Old_Town,_San_Diego,_CA,_USA_-_panoramio_(12).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Presidio_Park#Junípero_Serra_Museum" title="Presidio Park">Junípero Serra Museum</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Presidio_Park" title="Presidio Park">Presidio of San Diego</a>, California.</div></div></div>
<p>On June 28, sergeant <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Francisco_Ortega" title="José Francisco Ortega">José Ortega</a>, who had ridden ahead to meet the <a href="/wiki/Fernando_Rivera_y_Moncada" title="Fernando Rivera y Moncada">Rivera</a> party in San Diego, returned with fresh animals and letters to Serra from friars <a href="/wiki/Juan_Cresp%C3%AD" title="Juan Crespí">Juan Crespí</a> and Fernando Parrón. Serra learned that two Spanish <a href="/wiki/Galleon" title="Galleon">galleons</a> dispatched from Baja to supply the new missions had arrived at <a href="/wiki/San_Diego_Bay" title="San Diego Bay">San Diego Bay</a>. One of the ships, the <a href="/wiki/San_Carlos_(ship)" title="San Carlos (ship)"><i>San Carlos</i></a>, had sailed almost four months from <a href="/wiki/La_Paz,_Baja_California_Sur" title="La Paz, Baja California Sur">La Paz</a>, bypassing its destination by almost 200 miles before doubling back south to reach San Diego Bay.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> By the time it dropped anchor on April 29, <a href="/wiki/Scurvy" title="Scurvy">scurvy</a> had so devastated its crew that they lacked the strength to lower a boat. Men on shore from the <i>San Antonio</i>, which had arrived three weeks earlier, had to board the <i>San Carlos</i> to help its surviving crew ashore.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Portolá/Serra party, having trekked 900 miles (1,400 kilometers) from <a href="/wiki/Loreto,_Baja_California_Sur" title="Loreto, Baja California Sur">Loreto</a> and suffered dwindling food supplies along the way, arrived in <a href="/wiki/San_Diego" title="San Diego">San Diego</a> on July 1, 1769. "It was a day of great rejoicing and merriment for all," wrote Serra, "because although each one in his respective journey had undergone the same hardships, their meeting ... now became the material for mutual accounts of their experiences."<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between the overland and seafaring parties of the expedition, about 300 men had started on the trip from Baja California. But no more than half of them reached San Diego. Most of the Christian Indians recruited to the overland parties had died or deserted; military officers had denied them rations when food started running low. Half of those who made it to San Diego spent months unable to resume the expedition, due to illness.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Doctor Pedro Prat, who had also sailed on the <i>San Carlos</i> as the expedition's surgeon, struggled to treat the ill men, himself weakened from scurvy. Friar Fernando Parrón, who had sailed on the <i>San Carlos</i> as chaplain, had become weak with scurvy as well. Many men who had sailed on the <i>San Antonio</i>, including captain <a href="/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_P%C3%A9rez_Hern%C3%A1ndez" title="Juan José Pérez Hernández">Juan Pérez</a>, had also taken ill with scurvy.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Despite the efforts of Doctor Prat, many of the ill men died in San Diego.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal.C3.A1"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcalá">Mission San Diego de Alcalá</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/170px-Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="264" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/255px-Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/340px-Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2123" data-file-height="3299" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Junipero Serra at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">Mission San Diego de Alcalá</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>On July 16, 1769, Serra founded <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">mission San Diego</a> in honor of <a href="/wiki/Didacus_of_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Didacus of Alcalá">Didacus of Alcalá</a> in a simple shelter on Presidio Hill serving as a temporary church. Tensions with the local <a href="/wiki/Kumeyaay_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Kumeyaay people">Kumeyaay people</a> made it difficult to attract converts. The Indians accepted the trinkets Serra offered as rewards for visiting the new mission. But their craving for Spanish cloth irritated the soldiers, who accused them of stealing. Some of the Kumeyaay teased and taunted the sick soldiers. To warn them away, soldiers fired their guns into the air. The Christian Indians from Baja who remained with the Spaniards did not know the Kumeyaay language.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On August 15, the <a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary" title="Assumption of Mary">Feast of the Assumption</a>, Serra and padre Sebastian Vizcaíno celebrated Mass at the new mission chapel, to which several Hispanics had gone for confession and <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Holy Communion</a>. After Mass, four soldiers went down to the beach to bring padre Fernando Parrón back from the <i>San Carlos</i>, where he had been celebrating Mass.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Observing the mission and its neighboring huts sparsely protected, a group of over 20 Indians attacked with bows and arrows. The four remaining soldiers, aided by the blacksmith and carpenter, returned fire with muskets and pistols. Serra, clutching a Jesus figurine in one hand and a Mary figurine in the other, prayed to God to save both sides from casualties. The blacksmith, Chacón, ran about the Spanish huts unprotected by a leather jacket, shouting: "Long live the faith of Jesus Christ and may these dogs, enemies of that faith, die!"<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg/170px-Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="300" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg/255px-Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg/340px-Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg 2x" data-file-width="570" data-file-height="1007" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Serra_in_San_Diego_May_19_1770.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Illustration of Serra celebrating as the resupply ships the <i>San Antonio</i> and the <i>San Carlos</i> arrive at <a href="/wiki/San_Diego_Bay" title="San Diego Bay">San Diego Bay</a> on March 19, 1770.</div></div></div>
<p>Serra's young servant José María Vergerano ran into Serra's hut, his neck pierced by an arrow. "Father, absolve me," he beseeched, "for the Indians have killed me." "He entered my little hut with so much blood streaming from his temples and mouth that, shortly after, I gave him absolution and helped him to die well," wrote Serra. "He passed away at my feet, bathed in his blood."<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Padre Vizcaíno, the blacksmith Chacón, and a Christian Indian from <a href="/wiki/San_Ignacio,_Baja_California_Sur" title="San Ignacio, Baja California Sur">San Ignacio</a> suffered wounds. That night Serra buried Vergerano secretly, concealing his death from the Indians.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The Indian warriors, suffering several dead and wounded, retreated with a new-found respect for the power of Spanish firearms. As local Indians cremated their dead, the wailing of their women sounded from local villages. Yet Serra wrote six months later, in a letter to the guardian of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a>, that "both our men and theirs sustained wounds"—without mentioning any Indian deaths. He added: "It seems none of them died so they can still be baptized."<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Tightening security, the soldiers built a stockade of poles around the mission buildings, banning Indians from entering.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
