</p><p>A teenage boy from the <a href="/wiki/Kumeyaay_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Kumeyaay people">Kumeyaay</a> village of <a href="/wiki/Cosoy" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosoy">Kosa'aay</a> (Cosoy, known today as <a href="/wiki/Old_Town,_San_Diego" title="Old Town, San Diego">Old Town, San Diego</a>) who had often visited the mission before the outbreak of hostilities, resumed his visits with the friars. He soon learned enough Spanish for Serra to view him as an envoy to help convert the Kumeyaay. Serra urged the boy to persuade some parents to bring their young child to the mission, so that Serra could administer <a href="/wiki/Infant_baptism#Roman_Catholic_Church" title="Infant baptism">Catholic baptism</a> to the child by pouring water over his head.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>A few days later, a group of Indians arrived at the mission carrying a naked baby boy. The Spaniards interpreted their sign language as a desire to have the boy baptized. Serra covered the child with some clothing and asked the corporal of the guard to sponsor the baptism. Dressed in <a href="/wiki/Surplice" title="Surplice">surplice</a> and <a href="/wiki/Stole_(vestment)" title="Stole (vestment)">stole</a>, Serra read the initial prayers and performed the ceremonies to prepare for baptism. But just as he lifted the baptismal shell, filled it with water and readied to pour it over the baby's head, some Indians grabbed the child from the corporal's arms and ran away to their village in fear. The other Kumeyaay visitors followed them, laughing and jeering. The frustrated Serra never forgot this incident; recounting it years later brought tears to his eyes. Serra attributed the Indians' behavior to his own sins.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_(cropped).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG/170px-Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="175" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG/255px-Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG/340px-Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_%28cropped%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2898" data-file-height="2986" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Landing_place_of_Father_Junipero_Serra_(cropped).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Monument marking the landing place of Serra in <a href="/wiki/Monterey,_California" title="Monterey, California">Monterey, California</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Over six months dragged on without a single Indian convert to mission San Diego. On January 24, 1770, the 74 exhausted men of the <a href="/wiki/Portol%C3%A1_expedition" title="Portolá expedition">Portolá expedition</a> returned from their exploratory journey up the coast to San Francisco. They had survived by slaughtering and eating their mules along the return trek south. Commander <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Gaspar de Portolá</a>, engineer and cartographer <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Costans%C3%B3" title="Miguel Costansó">Miguel Costansó</a>, and friar <a href="/wiki/Juan_Cresp%C3%AD" title="Juan Crespí">Juan Crespí</a> all arrived in San Diego with detailed diaries of their trip. They reported large populations of Indians living along the coast who seemed friendly and docile, ready to embrace the gospel. Serra fervently wrote to the guardian of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a>, requesting more missionaries willing to face hardships in Alta California.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Food remained scarce as the San Diego outpost awaited the return of the supply ship <i>San Antonio</i>. Weighing the risk of his soldiers dying of starvation, Portolá set a deadline of March 19, the <a href="/wiki/St_Joseph%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St Joseph&#39;s Day">feast of saint Joseph</a>, patron of his expedition: If no ship arrived by that day—Portolá told Serra—he would march his men south the next morning. The anguished Serra, along with friar Juan Crespí, insisted on staying in San Diego in the event of the Portolá group's departure. Boarding the <i>San Carlos</i> (still anchored in San Diego Bay), Serra told captain Vicente Vila of Portolá's plan. Vila agreed to stay in the harbor until the relief ship arrived—and to welcome Serra and Crespí aboard if they got stranded by Portolá's departure.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On the morning of March 19, Serra celebrated Mass and preached a sermon at the forlorn mission on Presidio Hill. No ship appeared in the bay that morning. But around 3 o'clock in the afternoon, the sails of a ship—the <i>San Antonio</i>—came into view on the horizon. It sailed past San Diego Bay, destined for Monterey. When it got to the Santa Barbara Channel, its sailors made landfall to fetch fresh water. There they learned from Indians that the Portolá expedition had returned south. So the <i>San Antonio</i> also turned south, anchoring in San Diego Bay on March 23.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Monterey">Monterey</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg/220px-Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="161" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg/330px-Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg/440px-Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1697" data-file-height="1243" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Serra_Celebrates_Mass_at_Monterey_by_L%C3%A9on_Trousset.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Father Serra Celebrates Mass at Monterey</i>; painting by <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Trousset" title="Léon Trousset">Léon Trousset</a>, 1877.</div></div></div>
<p>Bolstered by the food unloaded from the <i>San Antonio</i>, Portolá and his men shifted their sights back north to <a href="/wiki/Monterey,_California" title="Monterey, California">Monterey</a>, specified by <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_G%C3%A1lvez" class="mw-redirect" title="José de Gálvez">José de Gálvez</a> as the northern stake of Spain's empire in California. Friar <a href="/wiki/Juan_Cresp%C3%AD" title="Juan Crespí">Juan Crespí</a> prepared to accompany the second Portolá expedition to Monterey. Leaving mission San Diego in the hands of friars Fernando Parrón and Francisco Gómez, Serra rode a launch out to board the <i>San Antonio</i>. He and Crespí would meet in Monterey. Since Serra planned to establish the mission there while having Crespí establish <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Buenaventura" title="Mission San Buenaventura">mission San Buenaventura</a>, the two friars would be living over 200 miles apart. "Truly," wrote Serra to <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pal%C3%B3u" title="Francisco Palóu">Palóu</a>, "this state of solitude shall be ... the greatest of my hardships, but God in His infinite mercy will see me through."<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On April 16, 1770, the <i>San Antonio</i> set sail from San Diego Bay, carrying Serra, doctor Pedro Prat, engineer <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Costans%C3%B3" title="Miguel Costansó">Miguel Costansó</a> and a crew of sailors under captain <a href="/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_P%C3%A9rez_Hern%C3%A1ndez" title="Juan José Pérez Hernández">Juan Pérez</a>. Contrary winds blew the ship back south to the <a href="/wiki/Baja_California_Peninsula" class="mw-redirect" title="Baja California Peninsula">Baja peninsula</a>, then as far north as the <a href="/wiki/Farallon_Islands" title="Farallon Islands">Farallon Islands</a>. As the ship heaved against heavy winds, Pérez, Serra and sailors recited daily prayers, promising to make a <a href="/wiki/Novena" title="Novena">novena</a> and celebrate High Mass upon their safe arrival in Monterey.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Several sailors fell sick with scurvy. Serra described the six-week voyage as "somewhat uncomfortable."<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Meanwhile, the land expedition departed from San Diego on April 17 under the command of <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Portolá</a>. His group included friar <a href="/wiki/Juan_Cresp%C3%AD" title="Juan Crespí">Crespí</a>, captain <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Fages" title="Pedro Fages">Pedro Fages</a>, twelve Spanish volunteers, seven leather-jacketed soldiers, two <a href="/wiki/Arriero" title="Arriero">muleteers</a>, five Baja Christian Indians, and Portolá's servant. Following the same route they had taken the year before, the expedition reached Monterey Bay on May 24, without losing a single man or suffering any serious illness.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak_after_1905,_Cathedral_of_San_Carlos_Borromeo_in_Monterey.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak_after_1905%2C_Cathedral_of_San_Carlos_Borromeo_in_Monterey.jpg/170px-Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak_after_1905%2C_Cathedral_of_San_Carlos_Borromeo_in_Monterey.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="240" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a9/Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak_after_1905%2C_Cathedral_of_San_Carlos_Borromeo_in_Monterey.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="246" data-file-height="347" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak_after_1905,_Cathedral_of_San_Carlos_Borromeo_in_Monterey.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Vizca%C3%ADno-Serra_Oak" title="Vizcaíno-Serra Oak">Vizcaíno-Serra Oak</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Monterey,_California" title="Monterey, California">Monterey</a>, where <a href="/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Vizca%C3%ADno" title="Sebastián Vizcaíno">Sebastián Vizcaíno</a> celebrated mass in 1602 and Serra celebrated mass in 1770.</div></div></div>
<p>With the <i>San Antonio</i> nowhere in sight, Portolá, Crespí and a guard walked over the hills to Point Pinos, then to a beachside hill just south where their party had planted a large cross five months before on their journey back from San Francisco Bay. They found the cross surrounded by feathers and broken arrows driven into the ground, with fresh sardines and meat laid out before the cross. No Indians were in sight. The three men then walked along the rocky coast south to <a href="/wiki/Carmel_Bay" title="Carmel Bay">Carmel Bay</a>. Several Indians approached them, and the two groups exchanged gifts.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> On May 31, the <i>San Antonio</i> sailed into <a href="/wiki/Monterey_Bay" title="Monterey Bay">Monterey Bay</a> and dropped anchor, reuniting the surviving men of the land and sea expeditions.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On <a href="/wiki/Pentecost" title="Pentecost">Pentecost</a> Sunday, June 3, 1770, Serra, Portolá and the whole expedition held a ceremony at a makeshift chapel erected next to a massive oak tree by Monterey Bay, to found <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">mission San Carlos Borromeo</a>. "The men of the land and sea expeditions coming from different directions met here at the same time," wrote Serra, "we singing the divine praises in our launch, while the gentlemen on land sang in their hearts." After the raising and planting of a large cross, which Serra blessed, "the standards of our Catholic monarch were also set up, the one ceremony ... accompanied by shouts of 'Long live the Faith!' and the other by 'Long live the King!' Added to this was the clangor of the bells, the volleys of the muskets, and the cannonading from the ship."<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Both king <a href="/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain" title="Charles III of Spain">Carlos III</a> and viceroy <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Francisco_de_Croix,_marqu%C3%A9s_de_Croix" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlos Francisco de Croix, marqués de Croix">Carlos de Croix</a> had chosen to name the new mission after <a href="/wiki/Charles_Borromeo" title="Charles Borromeo">Carlo Borromeo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> The body of a sailor, Alexo Niño, who had died the day before aboard the <i>San Antonio</i>, was buried at the foot of the newly erected cross.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra realized from the start that the new mission needed relocation: While the <a href="/wiki/Laws_of_the_Indies" title="Laws of the Indies">Laws of the Indies</a> required missions to be located near Indian villages, there were no Indian settlements near the newly christened mission by Monterey Bay. "It might be necessary," wrote Serra to the guardian of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_San_Fernando_de_Mexico" title="College of San Fernando de Mexico">college of San Fernando</a>, "to change the site of the mission toward the area of <a href="/wiki/Carmel-by-the-Sea,_California" title="Carmel-by-the-Sea, California">Carmel</a>, a locality indeed more delightful and suitable because of the extent and excellent quality of the land and water supply necessary to produce very abundant harvests."<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On July 9, the <i>San Antonio</i> set sail from Monterey, bound for Mexico. Aboard were <a href="/wiki/Gaspar_de_Portol%C3%A1" title="Gaspar de Portolá">Portolá</a> and <a href="/wiki/Miguel_Costans%C3%B3" title="Miguel Costansó">Miguel Costansó</a>, along with several letters from Serra. Forty men, including the two friars and five Baja Indians, remained to develop the mission on the <a href="/wiki/Monterey_peninsula" class="mw-redirect" title="Monterey peninsula">Monterey peninsula</a>. In San Diego, 450 miles (720 kilometers) south, 23 men remained to develop the mission there. Both groups would have to wait a year before receiving supplies and news from Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Missions_founded">Missions founded</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Diego,_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_(1).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg/170px-San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="123" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg/255px-San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg/340px-San_Diego%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_%281%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2432" data-file-height="1755" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Diego,_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcala_%5E_1769_%5E_-_1st_mission_-_panoramio_(1).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">Mission San Diego de Alcalá</a> was founded by Serra in 1769, as the first of the <a href="/wiki/California_missions" class="mw-redirect" title="California missions">California missions</a>.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg/170px-San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="178" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg/255px-San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg/340px-San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="947" data-file-height="989" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Carlos_Borromeo_Mission_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo del Río Caramelo</a>, where Serra died, was founded in 1770.</div></div></div>
<p>When the party reached <a href="/wiki/San_Diego,_California" class="mw-redirect" title="San Diego, California">San Diego</a> on July 1, Serra stayed behind to start the <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">Mission San Diego de Alcalá</a>, the first of the 21 California missions<sup id="cite_ref-Engelhardt_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Engelhardt-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> (including the nearby <a href="/wiki/Visita_de_la_Presentaci%C3%B3n" title="Visita de la Presentación">Visita de la Presentación</a>, also founded under Serra's leadership).
</p><p>Junipero Serra moved to the area that is now <a href="/wiki/Monterey,_California" title="Monterey, California">Monterey</a> in 1770, and founded <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borroméo de Carmelo</a>. He remained there as "Father Presidente" of the <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a> missions. In 1771, Serra relocated the mission to <a href="/wiki/Carmel-by-the-Sea,_California" title="Carmel-by-the-Sea, California">Carmel</a>, which became known as "Mission Carmel" and served as his headquarters.
Under his presidency were founded:<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mission_Basilica_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" class="mw-redirect" title="Mission Basilica San Diego de Alcalá">Mission Basilica San Diego de Alcalá</a>, July 16, 1769, present-day San Diego, California.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo</a>, June 3, 1770, present-day Carmel-by-the-Sea, California.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Antonio_de_Padua" title="Mission San Antonio de Padua">Mission San Antonio de Padua</a>, July 14, 1771, near present-day Jolon, California, was later converted into a <a href="/wiki/Parish_church" title="Parish church">parish church</a> and no longer provides any <a href="/wiki/Catholic_mission" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic mission">missions</a><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Gabriel_Arc%C3%A1ngel" title="Mission San Gabriel Arcángel">Mission San Gabriel Arcángel</a>, September 8, 1771, present-day San Gabriel, California.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Luis_Obispo_de_Tolosa" title="Mission San Luis Obispo de Tolosa">Mission San Luis Obispo de Tolosa</a>, September 1, 1772, present-day city of San Luis Obispo, California.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Juan_Capistrano" title="Mission San Juan Capistrano">Mission San Juan Capistrano</a>, November 1, 1776, present-day San Juan Capistrano</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Francisco_de_As%C3%ADs" title="Mission San Francisco de Asís">Mission San Francisco de Asís</a>, June 29, 1776, present-day San Francisco, California chain of missions.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_Santa_Clara_de_As%C3%ADs" title="Mission Santa Clara de Asís">Mission Santa Clara de Asís</a>, January 12, 1777, present-day city of Santa Clara, California, and</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Buenaventura" title="Mission San Buenaventura">Mission San Buenaventura</a>, March 31, 1782, present-day Ventura, California. Converted into a parish by 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Serra was also present at the founding of the <a href="/wiki/Presidio_of_Santa_Barbara" title="Presidio of Santa Barbara">Presidio of Santa Barbara</a> (Santa Barbara, California) on April 21, 1782, but was prevented from locating the mission there because of the animosity of Governor <a href="/wiki/Felipe_de_Neve" title="Felipe de Neve">Felipe de Neve</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_(cropped).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG/220px-Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="95" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG/330px-Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG/440px-Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_%28cropped%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2396" data-file-height="1040" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_San_Gabriel_4-15-05_6611_(cropped).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Gabriel_Arc%C3%A1ngel" title="Mission San Gabriel Arcángel">Mission San Gabriel Arcángel</a>, founded in 1771.</div></div></div>
<p>He began in San Diego on July 16, 1769, and established his headquarters near the <a href="/wiki/Presidio_of_Monterey,_California" title="Presidio of Monterey, California">Presidio of Monterey</a>, but soon moved a few miles south to establish <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo</a> in today's <a href="/wiki/Carmel,_California" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmel, California">Carmel, California</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SerraClub_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SerraClub-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg/220px-Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg/330px-Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg/440px-Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="360" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jewel_of_the_Missions.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Juan_Capistrano" title="Mission San Juan Capistrano">Mission San Juan Capistrano</a>, founded in 1776.</div></div></div>
<p>The missions were primarily designed to bring the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic faith">Catholic faith</a> to the native peoples. Other aims were to integrate the neophytes into Spanish society, to provide a framework for organizing the natives into a productive workforce in support of new extensions of Spanish power, and to train them to take over ownership and management of the land. As head of the order in California, Serra not only dealt with church officials, but also with Spanish officials in Mexico City and with the local military officers who commanded the nearby <a href="/wiki/Garrison" title="Garrison">garrison</a>.
</p><p>In 1773, difficulties with <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Fages" title="Pedro Fages">Pedro Fages</a>, the military commander, compelled Serra to travel to Mexico City to argue before <a href="/wiki/List_of_viceroys_of_New_Spain" title="List of viceroys of New Spain">Viceroy</a> <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Bucareli_y_Urs%C3%BAa" class="mw-redirect" title="Antonio María de Bucareli y Ursúa">Antonio María de Bucareli y Ursúa</a> for the removal of Fages as the Governor of California Nueva. At the capital of Mexico, by order of Viceroy Bucareli, he printed up <i>Representación</i> in 32 articles. Bucareli ruled in Serra's favor on 30 of the 32 charges brought against Fages, and removed him from office in 1774, after which time Serra returned to California. In 1778, Serra, although not a bishop, was given <a href="/wiki/Dispensation_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Dispensation (Catholic Church)">dispensation</a> to administer the sacrament of <a href="/wiki/Confirmation" title="Confirmation">confirmation</a> for the faithful in California. After he had exercised his privilege for a year, Governor <a href="/wiki/Felipe_de_Neve" title="Felipe de Neve">Felipe de Neve</a> directed him to suspend administering the sacrament until he could present the papal brief. For nearly two years Serra refrained, and then Viceroy Majorga gave instructions to the effect that Serra was within his rights.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Franciscans saw the Indians as children of God who deserved the opportunity for salvation, and would make good Christians. Converted Indians were segregated from Indians who had not yet embraced Christianity, lest there be a relapse. To understand the impetus behind missionary efforts in the 18th century, one must take into account the era's views on the salvation of unbaptized infants. While there were many controversies in the Church's history, the fate of unbaptized infants has never been definitively settled by an <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_council" title="Ecumenical council">ecumenical council</a> of Bishops in the Catholic church.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> 
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_Dolores_(1165072805).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Mission_Dolores_%281165072805%29.jpg/170px-Mission_Dolores_%281165072805%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="231" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Mission_Dolores_%281165072805%29.jpg/255px-Mission_Dolores_%281165072805%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/69/Mission_Dolores_%281165072805%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="294" data-file-height="400" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_Dolores_(1165072805).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Francisco_de_As%C3%ADs" title="Mission San Francisco de Asís">Mission San Francisco de Asís</a>, founded in 1776.</div></div></div>
<p>In the 18th century, most Catholic speculation regarding the ultimate end of unbaptized infants was still in line with the early <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a>, who believed that unbaptized infants would receive the mildest chastisements in Hell, but no reward.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> For Serra and his companions, therefore, instructing the natives so that their children might also be saved would have most likely been a great concern. From this came the determined efforts of missionaries to the detriment of native cultures, which few today would countenance.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Discipline was strict, and the converts were not allowed to come and go at will. Indians who were baptized were required to live at the mission and conscripted into forced labor as plowmen, shepherds, cattle herders, blacksmiths, and carpenters on the mission. Disease, starvation, overwork, and torture decimated these tribes.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 114">&#58;&#8202;114&#8202;</span></sup> Serra successfully resisted the efforts of Governor Felipe de Neve to bring Enlightenment policies to missionary work, because those policies would have subverted the economic and religious goals of the Franciscans.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra wielded this kind of influence because his missions served economic and political purposes as well as religious ends. The number of civilian colonists in Alta California never exceeded 3,200, and the missions with their Indian populations were critical to keeping the region within Spain's political orbit. Economically, the missions produced all of the colony's cattle and grain, and by the 1780s were even producing surpluses sufficient to trade with Mexico for luxury goods.<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1779, <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor" title="Order of Friars Minor">Franciscan</a> missionaries under Serra's direction planted California's first sustained <a href="/wiki/Vineyard" title="Vineyard">vineyard</a> at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">Mission San Diego de Alcalá</a>. Hence, he has been called the "Father of <a href="/wiki/History_of_California_wine" title="History of California wine">California Wine</a>". The variety he planted, presumably descended from Spain, became known as the <a href="/wiki/Mission_grape" class="mw-redirect" title="Mission grape">Mission grape</a> and dominated California wine production until about 1880.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Treatment_of_Native_Californians">Treatment of Native Californians</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Spanish_missions_in_California" title="Spanish missions in California">Spanish missions in California</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg/220px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg/330px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg/440px-Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2736" data-file-height="3648" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_National_Statuary_Hall_Collection.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Statue_of_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_(U.S._Capitol)" title="Statue of Junípero Serra (U.S. Capitol)">statue of Serra</a> by <a href="/wiki/Ettore_Cadorin" title="Ettore Cadorin">Ettore Cadorin</a> is one of two statues representing <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a> in the <a href="/wiki/National_Statuary_Hall" title="National Statuary Hall">National Statuary Hall</a> at the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol" title="United States Capitol">U.S. Capitol</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>From his perspective, Serra's singular purpose was to save the souls of <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">indigenous Americans</a>. He believed that the death of an unconverted heathen was tragic, while the death of a baptized convert was a cause for joy.<sup id="cite_ref-nugent_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nugent-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 39">&#58;&#8202;39&#8202;</span></sup> He maintained a patriarchal or fatherly attitude towards the Native American population. He wrote, "That spiritual fathers should punish their sons, the Indians, with blows appears to be as old as the conquest of the Americas; so general in fact that the saints do not seem to be any exception to the rule."<sup id="cite_ref-pbs_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pbs-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Punishment made clear to the natives "that we, every one of us, came here for the single purpose of doing them good and their eternal salvation."<sup id="cite_ref-nugent_101-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nugent-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 39">&#58;&#8202;39&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Serra also led efforts to protect the natives from abuse under Spanish soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> After a series of abuses on the native population by the hand of local soldiers, Serra and other missionaries protested against governor of Alta California <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Fages" title="Pedro Fages">Pedro Fages</a>, who refused to reprimand his soldiers. Serra then departed for Mexico on October 17, 1772 to plead his case to the viceroy <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Mar%C3%ADa_de_Bucareli_y_Urs%C3%BAa" class="mw-redirect" title="Antonio María de Bucareli y Ursúa">Antonio María de Bucareli y Ursúa</a>. Bucareli requested Serra set his grievances in writing, which led to the drafting of the Representación. This document, which consisted of 32-points, also laid out the rights of Native Americans in Spanish California and protections against the soldiers, placing them under the governance of the missions. Mission Indians enjoyed rights as human beings under the protection of the Spanish monarchy, and were recognized as Hijos de Dios, or "Children of God."<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> According to professor George Yagi, this stood in contrast to the treatment of Natives on the east coast of America, where they were guaranteed no rights.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Modern_controversy">Modern controversy</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Toppling_and_decapitation_of_Serra_statues">Toppling and decapitation of Serra statues</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_monuments_and_memorials_removed_during_the_George_Floyd_protests#Genocide_of_indigenous_peoples" title="List of monuments and memorials removed during the George Floyd protests">List of monuments and memorials removed during the George Floyd protests §&#160;Genocide of indigenous peoples</a></div>
<p>Native Americans objected to the Catholic Church's canonization of Serra, charging the priest "directed and approved of the torture and enslavement of Natives" at missions that served as both religious and military installations.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2015, a week after the Catholic Church canonized Serra, Serra's statue in <a href="/wiki/Monterey,_California" title="Monterey, California">Monterey</a> was decapitated.<sup id="cite_ref-Scheibe_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scheibe-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 12, 2017, Santa Barbara police reported a statue of Serra located at southern California's <a href="/wiki/Mission_Santa_Barbara" title="Mission Santa Barbara">Santa Barbara mission</a> had been decapitated and covered with red paint.<sup id="cite_ref-Scheibe_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scheibe-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On November 3, 2017, the statue of Serra located at the <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Gabriel_Arc%C3%A1ngel" title="Mission San Gabriel Arcángel">Mission San Gabriel Arcángel</a> was vandalized with red paint and suffered damage during a decapitation attempt with a reciprocating saw.<sup id="cite_ref-failedbeheading_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-failedbeheading-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Though the perpetrator failed to decapitate the bronze statue of Serra, $3000 was needed to repair it as well.<sup id="cite_ref-failedbeheading_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-failedbeheading-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On June 19, 2020, during the <a href="/wiki/George_Floyd_protests" title="George Floyd protests">worldwide civil unrest</a> that occurred after the <a href="/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd" title="Murder of George Floyd">murder of George Floyd</a>, activists in San Francisco's <a href="/wiki/Golden_Gate_Park" title="Golden Gate Park">Golden Gate Park</a> toppled a 30-foot replica of Serra, wrapped in a friar cloak and gripping a large cross. Once the statue fell red paint was poured on it and phrases including "Stolen Land," "Olone Land," and "Decolonize" were spray-painted on the pedestal where the founder of Spanish Missions previously stood.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On June 20, 2020, a crowd of indigenous elders and young people gathered in downtown Los Angeles to witness the toppling of Serra's statue at Father Serra Park near Olvera Street. Burning sage, speaking of their ancestors and chanting "Take it down!" the crowd watched as activists tied a rope around Serra's statue to rip it from its pedestal. Erected by the <a href="/wiki/Knights_of_Columbus" title="Knights of Columbus">Knights of Columbus</a> in 1932, the group said that the statue of Serra had become a symbol of Spanish colonization in which Native Americans, prohibited from practicing their customs and religion, were beaten when they tried to escape the church-run missions.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On July 4, 2020, a group of people toppled the statue of Serra located near the <a href="/wiki/California_State_Capitol" title="California State Capitol">California State Capitol</a> in Sacramento.<sup id="cite_ref-capitolgone_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-capitolgone-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> The group was among an estimated 200 protestors who marched through the streets of Sacramento.<sup id="cite_ref-capitolgone_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-capitolgone-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On October 12, 2020, a group of people toppled the statue of Serra located in front of <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Rafael_Arc%C3%A1ngel" title="Mission San Rafael Arcángel">Mission San Rafael Arcángel</a>, in <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael,_California" title="San Rafael, California">San Rafael, California</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Formal_renamings">Formal renamings</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_name_changes_due_to_the_George_Floyd_protests" title="List of name changes due to the George Floyd protests">List of name changes due to the George Floyd protests</a></div>
<p>On March 9, 2021, following a petition that began circulating after the murder of George Floyd in 2020, the <a href="/wiki/San_Diego_Unified_School_District" title="San Diego Unified School District">San Diego Unified School Board</a> formally renamed <a href="/wiki/Serra_High_School_(San_Diego)" class="mw-redirect" title="Serra High School (San Diego)">Serra High School</a> and its <a href="/wiki/Conquistador" title="Conquistador">Conquistador</a> mascot because of Serra's associations with indigenous assimilation.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversy_over_the_missions_Serra_operated">Controversy over the missions Serra operated</span></h4>
<p><i>The New York Times</i> noted that some "Indian historians and authors blame Father Serra for the suppression of their culture and the premature deaths at the missions of thousands of their ancestors."<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes2015_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes2015-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/George_Tinker" title="George Tinker">George Tinker</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Osage_Nation" title="Osage Nation">Osage</a>/<a href="/wiki/Cherokee" title="Cherokee">Cherokee</a> and professor at <a href="/wiki/Iliff_School_of_Theology" title="Iliff School of Theology">Iliff School of Theology</a> in <a href="/wiki/Denver" title="Denver">Denver</a>, <a href="/wiki/Colorado" title="Colorado">Colorado</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> cites evidence that Serra required the converted Indians to labor to support the missions. Tinker writes that while Serra's intentions in evangelizing were honest and genuine,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETinker199342_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETinker199342-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> overwhelming evidence suggests that the "native peoples resisted the Spanish intrusion from the beginning".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETinker199359_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETinker199359-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While administering Mission San Carlos Borromeo in Monterey, California, Serra had ongoing conflicts with <a href="/wiki/Pedro_Fages" title="Pedro Fages">Pedro Fages</a>, who was military governor of Alta California. Fages worked his men very harshly and was seen as a tyrant. Serra intervened on the soldiers' behalf, and the two did not get along.<sup id="cite_ref-walton_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-walton-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Serra moved the mission to Carmel due to better lands for farming, due to his conflicts with Fages, and in part to protect the Indian <a href="/wiki/Religious_conversion" title="Religious conversion">neophytes</a> from the influence of Spanish soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-Breschini_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Breschini-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Mark_A._Noll" class="mw-redirect" title="Mark A. Noll">Mark A. Noll</a>, a professor at <a href="/wiki/Wheaton_College_(Illinois)" title="Wheaton College (Illinois)">Wheaton College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Illinois" title="Illinois">Illinois</a>, wrote that Serra's attitude—that missionaries could, and should, treat their wards like children, including the use of corporal punishment—was common at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Tinker argues that it is more appropriate to judge the beatings and whippings administered by Serra and others from the point of view of the Native Americans, who were the victims of the violence, and who did not punish their children with physical discipline.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETinker199358_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETinker199358-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Salvatore_J._Cordileone" class="mw-redirect" title="Salvatore J. Cordileone">Salvatore J. Cordileone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">archbishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco" title="San Francisco">San Francisco</a>, acknowledges Native American concerns about Serra's whippings and coercive treatment, but argues that missionaries were also teaching school and farming.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes2015_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes2015-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg/170px-Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg/255px-Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg/340px-Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Junipero_Serra_late.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Serra statue at the <a href="/wiki/Presidio_of_Monterey" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidio of Monterey">Presidio of Monterey</a> was <a href="/wiki/Decapitation" title="Decapitation">decapitated</a> in 2015, but later repaired.</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Iris_Engstrand" title="Iris Engstrand">Iris Engstrand</a>, emerita professor of history at the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Catholic</a> <a href="/wiki/University_of_San_Diego" title="University of San Diego">University of San Diego</a>, described Serra as:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>much nicer to the Indians, really, than even to the governors. He didn't get along too well with some of the military people, you know. His attitude was, 'Stay away from the Indians'. I think you really come up with a benevolent, hard-working person who was strict in a lot of his doctrinal leanings and things like that, but not a person who was enslaving Indians, or beating them, ever. ... He was a very caring person and forgiving. Even after the burning of the <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Diego_de_Alcal%C3%A1" title="Mission San Diego de Alcalá">mission in San Diego</a>, he did not want those Indians punished. He wanted to be sure that they were treated fairly.<sup id="cite_ref-SerraClub_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SerraClub-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Serra wrote a letter in 1775 to <a href="/wiki/Fernando_Rivera_y_Moncada" title="Fernando Rivera y Moncada">Fernando Rivera y Moncada</a> explicitly instructing the colonial commander to whip and shackle Indigenous men who had escaped from Mission San Carlos:<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I am sending them to you so that a period of exile and two or three whippings which Your Lordship may order applied to them on different days may serve, for them and for the rest, for a warning, and may be of spiritual benefit to all. ... If Your Lordship does not have shackles, with your permission they may be sent from here.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Deborah_A._Miranda" title="Deborah A. Miranda">Deborah A. Miranda</a>, a professor of American literature at <a href="/wiki/Washington_and_Lee_University" title="Washington and Lee University">Washington and Lee University</a> and an enrolled member of the Ohlone/Costanoan Esselen Nation, stated that "Serra did not just bring us Christianity. He imposed it, giving us no choice in the matter. He did incalculable damage to a whole culture".<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes2015_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes2015-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Serra_statue_(Ventura,_California).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg/170px-Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="157" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg/255px-Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg/340px-Father_Serra_statue_%28Ventura%2C_California%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3002" data-file-height="2780" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Father_Serra_statue_(Ventura,_California).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Statues_of_Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_(Ventura,_California)" title="Statues of Junípero Serra (Ventura, California)">Father Serra statue</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ventura,_California" title="Ventura, California">Ventura, California</a></div></div></div>
<p>Professor Edward Castillo, a Native American and director of Native American Studies at the Sonoma State University in California, said in a <i><a href="/wiki/Firing_Line_(TV_program)" title="Firing Line (TV program)">Firing Line</a></i> episode with <a href="/wiki/William_F._Buckley_Jr." title="William F. Buckley Jr.">William F. Buckley Jr.</a> that "...&#160;you pointed out [that] in my work I haven't cited Serra as oppressor. You can't put a whip in his hand. You can't put a smoking gun in his hand. And that is true. The man was an administrator."<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Corine Fairbanks of the American Indian Movement proclaimed: "For too long the mission system has been glorified as these wonderful moments of California's golden era. That is not true. They were concentration camps. They were places of death.".<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a>, in addition to his canonization of Serra during a visit to the United States, called on Catholics to "embark upon a new chapter of evangelization."<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Francis further noted: "Instead of seeming to impose new obligations, (Christians) should appear as people who wish to share their joy, who point to a horizon of beauty and who invite others to a delicious banquet. It is not by proselytizing that the Church grows, but 'by attraction'."<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Catholic writers maintain that the attacks on Serra impose modern judgments about the appropriateness of Christian evangelization of non-Christians, and that much of the criticism leveled against Serra results from ahistorical value judgments and from ideologies that deny the validity of Christianity and Catholicism as a legitimate social and cultural force.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Accuracy_dispute#Disputed_statement" title="Wikipedia:Accuracy dispute"><span title="The material near this tag is possibly inaccurate or nonfactual. (July 2020)">dubious</span></a>&#32;<span class="metadata"> &#8211; <a href="/wiki/Talk:Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra#Dubious" title="Talk:Junípero Serra">discuss</a></span></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_for_canonization">Support for canonization</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez,_California_LCCN2013631418.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif/lossy-page1-170px-Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif/lossy-page1-255px-Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif/lossy-page1-340px-Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez%2C_California_LCCN2013631418.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4912" data-file-height="7360" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_Friar_Junipero_Serra_at_Santa_In%C3%A9s_Mission_in_Santa_Ynez,_California_LCCN2013631418.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of Junípero Serra at <a href="/wiki/Mission_Santa_In%C3%A9s" title="Mission Santa Inés">Mission Santa Inés</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Despite these concerns, thousands of Native Americans in California maintain their Catholic faith,<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> and some supported efforts to canonize Serra.<sup id="cite_ref-wsj.com_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wsj.com-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-latimes.com_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-latimes.com-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> James Nieblas—the first Native American priest to be ordained from the Juañeno Acjachemen Nation, a tribe evangelized by Serra—was chosen to meet with Pope Francis during his visit to Washington D.C.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Nieblas, a longtime supporter of Serra's canonization, stated during a 1986 interview with the <i>Los Angeles Times</i> that "Father Serra brought our people to this day. I think Serra would be proud ... canonization has the full support and backing of the Juaneno people."<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Members of other tribes associated with the mission system also expressed support for Serra's canonization. "Our people were directly involved with the Carmel Mission," said Tony Cerda, tribal chief of the Costanoan Rumsen Carmel tribe. "We support the canonization. ... The mission lands were our ancestral homes. Our ancestors are buried at the mission."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the Costanoan Rumsen Carmel Tribe's official website, the community released a bilingual statement in support of Serra's canonization shortly after a visit between Chief Cerda and Pope Francis, stating:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Saint Junipero Serra Baptized and Married our ancestors Simon Francisco (Indian name "Chanjay") and Magdalena Francisca on April 1, 1775 at Mission San Carlos De Borromeo Del Rio Carmelo ... We wholeheartedly Support the canonization of Saint Junipero Serra because he protected our people and supported their full human rights against the politicians and the military with total disregard for his own life and safety.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg/220px-2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg/330px-2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg/440px-2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="1467" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:2015-09-18_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_15.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra's coffin at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Two members of California's Ohlone Tribe played roles in the canonization Mass by placing a relic of Serra's near the altar and reading a scripture in Chochenyo, a native language. One of the participants, Andrew Galvan, a member of the Ohlone Tribe and curator of Mission Dolores in San Francisco who sat on the Junípero Serra Cause for Canonization board, stated prior to the ceremony that the canonization "will be the culmination of a life's work for me. ... It will be a ceremonial opening of the door that will 'let us Indians in,' a moment I honestly didn't think I would live to see."<sup id="cite_ref-wsj.com_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wsj.com-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ruben Mendoza, an archeologist of <a href="/wiki/Mexicans" title="Mexicans">Mexican</a> <a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">Mestizo</a> and Native <a href="/wiki/Yaqui" title="Yaqui">Yaqui</a> descent who has extensively excavated missions in California, stated during a March 2015 interview with the <i>Los Angeles Times</i> that "Serra endured great hardships to evangelize Native Californians. In the process, he orchestrated the development of a chain of missions that helped give birth to modern California. ... When I don't go along with the idea that the missions were concentration camps and that the Spanish brutalized every Indian they encountered, I'm seen as an adversary."<sup id="cite_ref-latimes.com_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-latimes.com-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:PCR" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:PCR"><span title="The material near this tag may lack sufficient context (February 2019)">needs context</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In July 2015, Mendoza testified at a hearing on a proposal to remove a statue of Junipero Serra from the U.S. Capitol. In his remarks, he stated, "What greater symbol of empowerment than that offered by Fray Junípero Serra himself can we offer our youth? I ask that this legislative body seriously reconsider this politicized effort to minimize and erase one of the most substantive <a href="/wiki/Hispanic" title="Hispanic">Hispanic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Latinos" class="mw-redirect" title="Latinos">Latino</a> contributions to our nation's history."<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Biographer Gregory Orfalea wrote of Serra: "I see his devotion to Native Californians as heartfelt, plain-spoken and borne out by continuous example."<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_(Carmel,_CA)_-_basilica,_interior,_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/170px-Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="302" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/255px-Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg/340px-Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_%28Carmel%2C_CA%29_-_basilica%2C_interior%2C_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1834" data-file-height="3263" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo_(Carmel,_CA)_-_basilica,_interior,_grave_of_Junipero_Serra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Junípero Serra's grave in <a href="/wiki/Carmel-by-the-Sea" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmel-by-the-Sea">Carmel-by-the-Sea</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>During the remaining three years of his life, he once more visited the missions from San Diego to San Francisco, traveling more than 600 miles in the process, to confirm all who had been baptized. He suffered intensely from his disabled leg and from his chest, yet he would use no remedies. He confirmed 5,309 people, who, with but few exceptions, were California Indian neophytes converted during the fourteen years from 1770.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On August 28, 1784, at the age of 70, Junípero Serra died at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo</a> from tuberculosis. He is buried there under the <a href="/wiki/Sanctuary" title="Sanctuary">sanctuary</a>. Following Serra's death, leadership of the Franciscan missionary effort in <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a> passed to <a href="/wiki/Ferm%C3%ADn_Lasu%C3%A9n" class="mw-redirect" title="Fermín Lasuén">Fermín Lasuén</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Carmel,_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_(3).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg/220px-Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg/330px-Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg/440px-Carmel%2C_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_%283%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2851" data-file-height="1900" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Carmel,_CA_USA_-_Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_-_panoramio_(3).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Serra's <a href="/wiki/Cenotaph" title="Cenotaph">cenotaph</a> at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Carlos_Borromeo_de_Carmelo" title="Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo">Mission San Carlos Borromeo de Carmelo</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Junípero Serra was beatified by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> on September 25, 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The pope spoke before a crowd of 20,000 in a beatification ceremony for six; according to the pope's address in English, "He sowed the seeds of Christian faith amid the momentous changes wrought by the arrival of European settlers in the New World. It was a field of missionary endeavor that required patience, perseverance, and humility, as well as vision and courage."<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During Serra's beatification, questions were raised about how Indians were treated while Serra was in charge. The question of Franciscan treatment of Indians first arose in 1783. The famous historian of missions <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Eugene_Bolton" title="Herbert Eugene Bolton">Herbert Eugene Bolton</a> gave evidence favorable to the case in 1948, and the testimony of five other historians was solicited in 1986.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serra was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Francis" title="Pope Francis">Pope Francis</a> on September 23, 2015, as a part of the pope's first visit to the United States,<sup id="cite_ref-Sep2015_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sep2015-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> the first canonization to take place on American soil.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> During a speech at the Pontifical North American College in Rome on May 2, 2015, Pope Francis stated that "Friar Junípero ... was one of the founding fathers of the United States, a saintly example of the Church's universality and special patron of the Hispanic people of the country."<sup id="cite_ref-en.radiovaticana.va_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-en.radiovaticana.va-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Junípero Serra is the second native saint of the Balearic Islands after <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Palma" title="Catherine of Palma">Catherine of Palma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Sep2015_147-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sep2015-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> He is also included among the Saints of the United States and México.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Serra's <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is celebrated on July 1 in the United States and on August 28 everywhere.<sup id="cite_ref-faithnd_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-faithnd-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CatholicSaints.Info_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CatholicSaints.Info-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RomanCatholicSaints_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RomanCatholicSaints-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He is considered to be the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of California, <a href="/wiki/Hispanic_Americans" class="mw-redirect" title="Hispanic Americans">Hispanic Americans</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Religious_vocation" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious vocation">religious vocations</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The Mission in Carmel, California, containing Serra's remains has continued as a place of public veneration. The burial location of Serra is southeast of the altar and is marked with an inscription in the floor of the sanctuary. Other relics are remnants of the wood from Serra's coffin on display next to the sanctuary, and personal items belonging to Serra on display in the mission museums. A bronze and marble sarcophagus depicting Serra's life was completed in 1924 by the sculptor <a href="/wiki/Jo_Mora" title="Jo Mora">Jo Mora</a>, but Serra's remains have never been transferred to that sarcophagus.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego,_California.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg/220px-Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg/330px-Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg/440px-Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego%2C_California.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3012" data-file-height="2009" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Balboa_Park_in_San_Diego,_California.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Architectural medallion venerating Serra at <a href="/wiki/Balboa_Park_(San_Diego)" title="Balboa Park (San Diego)">Balboa Park</a> in <a href="/wiki/San_Diego" title="San Diego">San Diego</a>.</div></div></div> 
<p>Many of Serra's letters and other documentation are extant, the principal ones being his <i>"Diario"</i> of the journey from Loreto to San Diego, which was published in <i>Out West</i> (March to June 1902) along with Serra's <i>Representación</i>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The Junípero Serra Collection (1713–1947) at the <a href="/wiki/Mission_Santa_Barbara" title="Mission Santa Barbara">Santa Barbara Mission Archive-Library</a> are their earliest archival materials. This library is part of the building complex of the <a href="/wiki/Mission_Santa_Barbara" title="Mission Santa Barbara">Mission Santa Barbara</a>, but is now a separate non-profit, independent educational and research institution. It continues to have ties to the Franciscans and the legacy of Serra.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The chapel at <a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Juan_Capistrano" title="Mission San Juan Capistrano">Mission San Juan Capistrano</a>, built in 1782, is thought to be the oldest standing building in California. Commonly referred to as "Father Serra's Church,"<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> it is the only remaining church in which Serra is known to have celebrated the rites of the Roman Catholic Church (he presided over the confirmations of 213 people on October 12 and 13, 1783).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg/170px-Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg/255px-Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg/340px-Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg 2x" data-file-width="890" data-file-height="1334" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Serra_Statue_at_Serra_HS.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue at <a href="/wiki/Jun%C3%ADpero_Serra_High_School_(San_Mateo,_California)" title="Junípero Serra High School (San Mateo, California)">Junípero Serra High School</a> in <a href="/wiki/San_Mateo,_California" title="San Mateo, California">San Mateo</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Many cities in California have streets, schools, and other features named after Serra. Examples include <a href="/wiki/Junipero_Serra_Boulevard" title="Junipero Serra Boulevard">Junipero Serra Boulevard</a>, a major boulevard in and south of San Francisco; <a href="/wiki/Serramonte" title="Serramonte">Serramonte</a>, a large 1960s residential neighborhood on the border of Daly City and Colma in the suburbs south of San Francisco; <a href="/wiki/Serra_Springs_(California)" class="mw-redirect" title="Serra Springs (California)">Serra Springs</a>, a pair of springs in Los Angeles; <a href="/wiki/Serra_Mesa,_San_Diego" title="Serra Mesa, San Diego">Serra Mesa</a>, a community in San Diego; <a href="/wiki/Junipero_Serra_Peak" title="Junipero Serra Peak">Junipero Serra Peak</a>, the highest mountain in the Santa Lucia Mountains; <a href="/wiki/Junipero_Serra_Landfill" title="Junipero Serra Landfill">Junipero Serra Landfill</a>, a solid waste disposal site in Colma; and <a href="/wiki/Serra_Fault" title="Serra Fault">Serra Fault</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Fault_(geology)" title="Fault (geology)">fault</a> in San Mateo County.
